\end{corollary}

This says nothing about the unrestricted real domain, where negative selectors invalidate the nonnegativity argument. An earlier factorized lookup remains an independently tested baseline: three $q$-selectors, $q^2$ pair products, and $q^3$ triple products give
\[
 V_{\mathrm{fact}}=T[M(q^3+q^2+3q+14)+5M(M-1)],
\]
\[
 R_{\mathrm{fact}}=T[M(q^3+q^2+19)+5M(M-1)].
\]
Its orthant strengthening costs $4MT$ residuals. These are baseline counts, not the preferred single-row-simplex ledger in~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:V}--\eqref{smc:sl:eq:R}.
\section{The reversible conservative source interface}\label{smc:sl:sec:source}
Morita and Imai~\cite{moritaimai} construct a reversible number-conserving partitioned automaton from a reversible two-counter machine. The following source audit fixes the exact contract used here. It distinguishes the published theorem, a correction forced by its encoding, and an additional reachable-legality argument needed when unspecified local rows are completed arbitrarily.

\subsection{Source syntax and encoding}
A transition is $(i,j,\mathsf{op},k)$, with source state $q_i$, target $q_k$, counter $j\in\{0,1\}$, and operation increment, decrement, no-op, zero test, or positive test. There are $m\ge2$ states, $q_0$ has no incoming transition, and $q_{m-1}$ is designated final with no outgoing transition. Distinct transitions with the same source may only be complementary zero/positive tests on the same counter; the same restriction applies to distinct transitions with the same target. This is the paper's syntactic deterministic and reversible model, a stronger specification than a vague assertion of injectivity for some other instruction language.

Put
\[
 a_i=i+10,\qquad B=m+16,\qquad b_i=B-a_i-\gamma_i,
\]
where $\gamma_i=2,4,6,7$ for increment counter zero, decrement counter zero, increment counter one, decrement counter one respectively, and zero otherwise. The alphabet cap is $K=m+18$ and the number of ordinary cell states is $(m+19)^3$.

The encoding $\phi(q_i,n_0,n_1)$ places $(a_i,b_i,\gamma_i)$ at the origin. Counter $j$ contributes marker mass $2^j$ at position $n_j$, normally in the center lane. If $n_j=0$ and $q_i$ increments that counter, its marker is instead added to the right lane at the origin. Contributions at the same site add. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{smc:sl:eq:source-mass}
 M=B+1+2=m+19=q,
\end{equation}
independently of counter magnitude. The support occupies at most three sites, all in $\{0,n_0,n_1\}$. Coincident markers have total three, retaining their separate zero/positive information through distinct weights. Input size is paid through distances and their bit lengths, not a variable number of particles.

\subsection{The printed sign defect and the repaired local injection}
On printed page 249, rule (8.2) has an output-center $+\gamma_k$. When a no-op enters a state incrementing counter $h$, the input and repaired output are
\begin{align}
 &(a_i,\ B-a_i+c2^h+c'2^{1-h},\ 0)\nonumber\\
 &\hspace{2em}\longmapsto
 (a_k,\ B-a_k-\gamma_k+c'2^{1-h},\ \gamma_k+c2^h),
 \qquad c,c'\in\{0,1\}.\label{smc:sl:eq:repair}
\end{align}
The printed plus would increase the local total by $2\gamma_k$, namely four or twelve. The minus conserves mass and gives exactly the declared target encoding. This is a necessary correction to that printed formula; no author-issued erratum is asserted. The target's increment counter $h$ must also be read from $q_k$, rather than borrowed from the no-op quadruple's semantically irrelevant counter tag. Opposite-tag cases are included in the executable audit.

\wtag\ The repository's Hilbert-tenth-problem programme checked this sign against the primary paper (printed page~249 of~\cite{moritaimai}) in its review of source~13~\cite{reviewsl}: subtracting the target opcode is forced by mass conservation and the target encoding, and the target's increment counter, not the no-op tag, controls the marker routing. Like source~13, the review records a mathematical correction to a published formula, not an author erratum. No report or README of the repository made a claim that this correction refutes.

For clarity, the structural proof behind finite row checks is as follows. The ordinary signal rules route signal mass or transfer a marker between center and signal; waiting rows are identities. After~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:repair}, state-update rows preserve $B$ plus the zero-marker mask. Startup and final rows exchange a unit signal plus a reservoir $m+15$ with control mass $B$. For nonfinal $i$, the smallest baseline obeys
\[
 b_i\ge m+6-(m-2)-7=1;
\]
the final baseline is seven. Added marker mass is at most three, all center values are at most $m+18$, and state signals are at most $m+9$. Thus all coordinates stay in range.

Routine signal domains differ from control domains by their nonzero lane pattern and by signal values below or above ten. At a fixed control state its unique operation type chooses the rule family. Zero masks are distinct; complementary test branches have disjoint masks. The no-outgoing convention at the final state removes domain conflicts there. This proves the function property.

For injection, different target state signals have different left components. At a fixed target, its center/right pair recovers the zero-mask bits: an increment target carries its own zero marker in the right signal and leaves the other bit in the center; otherwise both bits remain centered. A target with a movement or no-op predecessor has only that incoming transition. If it has two incoming tests, they test the same counter with complementary outcomes and hence disjoint masks. The \emph{whole output triple} distinguishes them, even when the center alone does not. Waiting outputs have right component zero, whereas a transition into an operation state has a positive right signal. Startup targets $q_0$, which has no incoming source transition, and the final unit signal with its large reservoir is distinct from an ordinary travelling unit signal. The remaining routine signal output patterns are disjoint by lane and signal type. Together these observations establish a mass-preserving partial injection.

\begin{lemma}[Completion within mass fibers]\label{smc:sl:lem:completion}
Every finite mass-preserving partial injection on the local triple alphabet extends effectively to a mass-preserving permutation. This applies to the repaired table.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For each $s$, let $\Omega_s$ be the triples with coordinate sum $s$. Used inputs and used outputs have equal cardinality within $\Omega_s$. Bijection of their unused complements, for example by pairing lexicographically sorted lists, completes this fiber. Their union is the required permutation. The unique weight-zero triple is fixed. Lane transport followed by the local permutation is a global reversible conservative CA.
\end{proof}
Simply assigning identity to every unspecified input is generally wrong. The specified row $(2,1,0)\mapsto(0,0,3)$ can leave $(0,0,3)$ unspecified as an input. Its identity image would duplicate an already used output. Also, Lemma~\ref{smc:sl:lem:completion} is about a partitioned/block local permutation; it is not a completion theorem for arbitrary partial ordinary-neighborhood CA rules.

\subsection{Exact source microtimes and the pulse observer}
For a \emph{legal} source step, the repaired specified rows, without using any completion row, carry $\phi(I)$ to $\phi(J)$ in the following number of microsteps:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}
\toprule
Source operation on a counter of value $n$ & Microsteps\\\midrule
Increment, including $n=0$ & $2n+2$\\
Decrement, with $n>0$ & $2n$\\
No-op or legal zero/positive test & $2$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Here is the geometric reason for the clocks. The state signal travels from the origin to site $-1$ and back, while the control waits for its operation signal. An increment signal traverses the distance to its marker, carries that marker one step farther right, deposits it, and returns to the origin, a total of $2n+2$ steps. When $n=0$, the marker is already in the initial right signal, giving the same two-step formula. A decrement signal reaches its positive-position marker, reflects while carrying it one step left, deposits it, and returns in $2n$ steps. At $n=1$ the returning marker joins the origin's zero mask directly. The other counter's marker is transparent to these routes, including coincident marker positions, because the rule families carry its distinct mass unchanged. Tests and no-ops only require the state signal's two-step return. The state-update rows then redistribute the zero mask into exactly the target encoding. These routes also explain why no constant slowdown is promised.

Let $t_h$ be the sum of these times up to first entry into $q_f=q_{m-1}$. From $\phi(q_f,n_0,n_1)$ the state signal is in the right lane at $-1$ at $t_h+1$; the final rule creates a left unit at the origin at $t_h+2$; and
\begin{equation}\label{smc:sl:eq:haltpulse}
 L_{t_h+3}(-1)=1.
\end{equation}
That unit subsequently moves left forever. Before final entry, the specified simulation trajectory never has this left-unit signal. After its departure, the fixed centers are
\[
 C(0)=m+15+\ind{n_0=0}+2\ind{n_1=0},
 \qquad C(x)=\ind{x=n_0}+2\ind{x=n_1}\quad(x>0).
\]
All right channels are zero. The results remain readable while information escapes spatially. This is an anchored pulse, not an absorbing globally halted configuration. The complete successful orbit has unbounded diameter.

The equivalence between this observer and source halting requires that \emph{every reachable nonfinal source configuration on the input under discussion has a legal next instruction}. This is called reachable legality below. It is not asserted for every arbitrary counter pair at every internal state.

\subsection{An explicit false observer on an unsafe source}
Take $m=2$ and the sole source instruction ``decrement counter zero from $q_0$ to $q_1$.'' On counters $(0,0)$ that instruction is illegal, so the designated final state is not reached. Its encoded initial CA state is $(10,7,4)$ at site zero, of mass 21. At time one the nonzero sites are
\[
 -1:(0,0,10),\qquad 0:(0,7,0),\qquad 1:(0,0,4).
\]
The next origin input $(10,7,0)$ is unspecified. Both this input and the output $(1,16,0)$ are unused, and both have mass 17, so adding
\[
 (10,7,0)\longmapsto(1,16,0)
\]
is compatible with Lemma~\ref{smc:sl:lem:completion}. At time two the configuration is $0:(1,16,0)$ and $2:(0,0,4)$; at time three it has a left unit at $-1$, falsely satisfying the proposed observer. The release supplies the entire resulting 9,261-row permutation and a replay through time eight.

Thus an arbitrary partial reversible source and an arbitrary conservative permutation completion do not provide a halting equivalence on every encoded pair. Missing test branches and unnamed dead states have the same logical risk. This example does not affect the generic theorem, which always certifies the actual supplied CA dynamics, and does not refute a safe universal-source construction.

\section{Safe universal sources and the paid loader}\label{smc:sl:sec:safe}
\subsection{The existence argument and its exact scope}
Morita's 1996 theorems~\cite{morita1996} give designated-final-state simulation equivalences. The following preservation of reachable legality is an explicit invariant argument through those constructions, rather than an attribution of a separate totality theorem to that paper.

Start with a fixed universal deterministic Turing machine whose transition is defined at every nonhalting state/symbol pair. On its computably valid program/input encodings, choose a complete guarded counter simulation: decrement is entered only after positivity, every appropriate test branch exists, and each arithmetic routine terminates on its finite inputs. A fresh initial state and a terminal designated final state give the required endpoint conventions. This yields a safe irreversible counter simulator. Splitting high-indegree target states through finite no-op merge chains, as in Morita's preparatory reduction, preserves legal progress: one legal step only acquires a finite chain of unconditional steps.

The reversible history compiler adds a history counter $H$ and scratch counter $W$. At an original-state boundary the invariant is
\[
 (\hbox{simulated counters correct}),\qquad H\in\NN,\qquad W=0.
\]
An already reversible incoming relation is copied. For an overlapping incoming pair, the compiler takes the original legal operation and then records which predecessor was used. It transfers $H$ to $W$ through positivity-guarded decrements and reconstructs $2H$ or $2H+1$ in the history counter while emptying $W$. Each decrement is legal by its positive guard; required positive back-edge tests follow increments; each transfer loop terminates by decreasing its source counter. The boundary invariant is restored at the target. Thus a legal step expands into a finite nonempty legal path, without a new nonfinal dead end. Erasing history is unnecessary for this existence argument.

The reversible multi-counter machine is then represented by two counters
\begin{equation}\label{smc:sl:eq:prime}
 (N,S)=\left(\prod_{i=1}^k p_i^{v_i},0\right),
 \qquad N\ge1,
\end{equation}
where the $p_i$ are distinct fixed primes. This is a constrained encoding of all data, history, and scratch counters, not an arbitrary pair in $\NN^2$. The prime compiler has the following legal-progress invariants.
\begin{itemize}
\item An increment of counter $i$ transfers $N$ to scratch through guarded decrements and reconstructs $p_iN$ while consuming scratch. Both loops terminate and end with scratch zero
\item A legal decrement has $v_i>0$, so $p_i\mid N$. After transfer, each positive scratch batch has at least $p_i$ units. Its $p_i$ successive decrements are legal even where only the first is explicitly guarded. Consuming all batches gives $(N/p_i,0)$
\item A test computes quotient and residue by a guarded finite-state division loop. Divisibility by $p_i$ is exactly $v_i>0$; nonzero residue is exactly $v_i=0$. The appropriate source branch is selected, and quotient and residue restore the original $N$ through terminating positively guarded loops
\item A no-op is copied. If a source has only one test exit, the omitted exit is never needed on its legal encoded trajectory
\end{itemize}
The original state, prime encoding, and scratch-zero invariant hold again at each boundary. Fresh internal states preserve the source's required reversible incoming syntax and initial/final conventions. Induction proves that every reachable nonfinal primitive two-counter state on a valid input has a successor. If the original machine never halts, each of its steps expands into a nonempty finite legal path, so no stuck intermediate configuration can masquerade as a halt.

Fixing the universal program fixes the resulting finite two-counter source and hence, by the corrected local construction and a chosen completion, one finite reversible conservative CA. Its valid program/data encodings vary only through the initial counter positions. Eventual anchored pulse occurrence is therefore computably enumerable hard on this computable valid-input family; direct simulation makes it computably enumerable. This supplies the usual undecidable local-observation problem for a fixed finite-mass substrate.

The release does not materialize a chosen universal source table or give its numerical $m$. It does not claim faithful source interpretation for all $(n_0,n_1)\in\NN^2$, intrinsic CA universality, or an efficient simulation. In particular, the often cited 96-state construction using an ultimately periodic infinite background is not a finite-mass substitute. Exact-target reachability is also a different question: the final counter positions need not be computable in advance, and the escaping halt signal is not a specified complete target. Transferring exact-target undecidability would require an additional normal form or compute/uncompute argument.

\subsection{A uniform initial loader with no hidden input oracle}
For fixed source data $m$, initial opcode $\gamma_0$, and fixed $T$, leave $n_0,n_1\in\NN$ as free polynomial parameters. At position $T$ form ten left records, $m+6-\gamma_0$ center records, and $\gamma_0$ right records. Add one counter-zero record at $n_0+T$ and two counter-one records at $n_1+T$. A marker's channel is right precisely when its position parameter is zero and $q_0$ increments that counter; otherwise it is center.

Two comparisons $\CMP(n_j,0)$ determine the zero flags, costing four witnesses and four residuals. The initial list is sorted by the same network as before. Its exact loader cost is
\begin{equation}\label{smc:sl:eq:loader}
 V_{\mathrm{load}}=R_{\mathrm{load}}=4+3M(M-1).
\end{equation}
Even unused zero tests are included in this declared ledger. The exported residual coefficients are independent of the supplied values of $n_0,n_1$; only those parameter values and the canonical witnesses vary. Bound~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:H} remains valid with $D=\max(n_0,n_1)$, including loader comparison slacks.

\subsection{Paying for the exact first pulse}
For $T\ge1$, site $-1$ is shifted to $T-1$. For each $t=1,\ldots,T$ and each record, compute $\EQ(x_{t,i},T-1)$. Reuse the channel one-hot vectors already present at $t<T$, and add $3M$ channel witnesses with $2M$ residuals for the final row. Add one residual at each time,
\begin{equation}\label{smc:sl:eq:observe}
 \sum_i e(x_{t,i},T-1)L_{t,i}-\ind{t=T}.
\end{equation}
This forces detector left mass zero at every earlier observed time and one at $T$. The initial detector is blank because $\phi$ is supported on nonnegative sites. The additional cost is exactly
\begin{align}
 V_{\mathrm{obs}}&=4MT+3M,\label{smc:sl:eq:obsV}\\
 R_{\mathrm{obs}}&=4MT+2M+T.\label{smc:sl:eq:obsR}
\end{align}
For the orthant-exact variant add $M$ final decoder norm residuals, on top of the core $2MT$ extra residuals. No observation witnesses are needed for a fixed endpoint beyond the core row witnesses, but that is a different predicate.

These guards mean \emph{zero before and one now}. For an arbitrary CA they are not the generic first occurrence of the value one, because earlier detector masses greater than one are also excluded. On valid source simulations they match the exact unique pulse in~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:haltpulse}. Combining~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:V}, \eqref{smc:sl:eq:R}, \eqref{smc:sl:eq:loader}, \eqref{smc:sl:eq:obsV}, and~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:obsR} gives the full paid uniform-input first-pulse certificate. Safe-source legality concerns the semantic interpretation of that predicate, not the arithmetic soundness of the actual-CA certificate.

\subsection{The fully specified doubling example}\label{smc:sl:sec:doubling}
The worked source has seven states and the following complete transition list; all other source transitions are absent:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}cccc@{}}
\toprule
Source & Counter & Operation & Target\\\midrule
$q_0$ & $1$ & zero test & $q_1$\\
$q_1$ & $0$ & zero test & $q_6$\\
$q_1$ & $0$ & positive test & $q_2$\\
$q_2$ & $0$ & decrement & $q_3$\\
$q_3$ & $1$ & increment & $q_4$\\
$q_4$ & $1$ & increment & $q_5$\\
$q_5$ & $1$ & positive test & $q_1$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
On $(q_0,n,0)$ every reachable nonfinal instruction is legal: the first test sees zero, each decrement follows a positive counter-zero test, and the last positive test follows two increments. The machine reaches $(q_6,0,2n)$ after $5n+2$ source steps. Summing the exact microclocks gives
\begin{equation}\label{smc:sl:eq:doublingtime}
 t_h=5n^2+7n+4,\qquad t_{\mathrm{pulse}}=5n^2+7n+7.
\end{equation}
Indeed iteration $k=0,\ldots,n-1$ takes a positive test, a decrement of $n-k$, increments at $2k$ and $2k+1$, and a final test, totalling $2n+6k+10$ microsteps. The initial and final zero tests add four; summing yields~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:doublingtime}.

Here $K=25$, $M=q=26$. The corrected specified local table has 97 distinct rows, and its lexicographic mass-fiber completion has all $26^3=17{,}576$ rows. For $n=2$, twelve source steps reach $\phi(q_6,0,4)$ at time 38, create a left unit at the origin at time 40, and produce the detector pulse at time 41. The full table and trajectory checks are shipped. This explicit example verifies the interface and its costs without representing a universal-machine claim.
\section{Geometric barriers and translating periodicity}\label{smc:sl:sec:diameter}
The next results use a more general model than the triple-channel compiler. Let $Q$ be a finite alphabet, $0$ a quiescent state, and $F:Q^\Z\to Q^\Z$ a deterministic translation-invariant CA of radius $r$. Let
\[
 w:Q\to\NN,\qquad w(0)=0,\qquad w(a)\ge1\quad(a\ne0),
\]
conserve total weight on every finite-support configuration. Thus the vacuum is the unique zero-weight symbol. A mass-$M$ configuration has at most $M$ occupied sites. Distinct states may have the same positive weight; this matters when comparing particle bounds in numerical-state models.

Zero-weight active symbols, signed weights allowing cancellation, and a nonzero infinite periodic background are outside these hypotheses. Quiescence and locality preserve finite support at each finite time, but do not bound its position or its diameter uniformly in time. A finite-support configuration on $\Z$ is not a finite-ring configuration.

Write $(\mathcal T_a x)(i)=x(i-a)$ for translation to the right. For nonempty $x$, let $\ell(x)=\min\supp(x)$ and $\norm{x}=\mathcal T_{-\ell(x)}x$ be its normalized shape. The vacuum is handled separately.

\begin{proposition}[Deciding a diameter bound and its tail]\label{smc:sl:prop:bounded}
Given $F$, a finite configuration $x$, and an integer $D\ge0$, one can decide whether $\diam(F^t x)\le D$ for every $t\ge0$. Under this full-orbit bound, exact finite-configuration reachability and finite-word occurrence, anchored or at any translate, are decidable. Without the bound promise, the same procedure decides occurrence before the first escape and otherwise reports the escape, without concluding anything about later unrestricted occurrence.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
There are finitely many normalized nonempty shapes of diameter at most $D$, at most $(|Q|-1)|Q|^D$. With mass $M$ a sharper bound is
\begin{equation}\label{smc:sl:eq:shape-count}
 \sum_{k=1}^{\min(M,D+1)}\binom D{k-1}(|Q|-1)^k.
\end{equation}
Position zero is occupied, the remaining $k-1$ positions are chosen in $\{1,\ldots,D\}$, and all occupied symbols are nonzero. An exact-weight count is the coefficient of $z^M$ in
\[
 \left(\sum_{a\ne0}z^{w(a)}\right)
 \left(\sum_{a\in Q}z^{w(a)}\right)^D.
\]
Simulate until the diameter exceeds $D$ or a normalized shape repeats. One event must happen in finitely many steps. If
\[
 \norm{F^i x}=\norm{F^j x},\quad i<j,
 \qquad p=j-i,\quad \delta=\ell(F^j x)-\ell(F^i x),
\]
then determinism and translation equivariance give, for every $0\le s<p$ and $k\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{smc:sl:eq:translate-tail}
 F^{i+s+kp}x=\mathcal T_{k\delta}F^{i+s}x.
\end{equation}
Consequently no future shape escapes. An escape disproves the diameter bound, while a repeat proves it.

For exact target $y$, inspect the finite prefix and then each phase $z=F^{i+s}x$. It can reach $y$ exactly when its normalized shape agrees and
\[
 \ell(y)-\ell(z)=k\delta\quad\hbox{for an integer }k\ge0.
\]
This is a divisibility and sign test; when $\delta=0$ it is simply equality of offsets.

For a full word on a fixed interval, each occupied phase site moves as $u+k\delta$. Required nonzero symbols give position equalities and symbol tests; required zeros exclude the moving occupied sites from their positions. The resulting finite Boolean combination of affine equalities and inequalities in one integer $k\ge0$ is decidable, including an all-zero word. For occurrence anywhere, translation of the entire phase does not change the answer, so a finite prefix and one list of phases suffice. A local predicate allowing several alphabet symbols is a finite union of these tests.

If an escape is encountered before an observed hit, it only disproves the bound. It does not reject a target that might appear after the escape. This is why the unrestricted-reachability conclusion requires the stated full-orbit promise.
\end{proof}

If $F$ is injective on finite configurations, a normalized repeat has no true transient. From $F^j x=\mathcal T_\delta F^i x$ and equivariance, cancel $F^i$ to obtain $F^{j-i}x=\mathcal T_\delta x$. Thus a wholly bounded-diameter reversible orbit is translating periodic from time zero. If the existence of some diameter bound is promised but no value is supplied, simply simulate normalized shapes until a repeat; the promise ensures termination. Without that promise this is only a semidecision of boundedness.

These arguments are elementary single-orbit consequences of finite normalized shape space. Classical number-conserving CA dynamics already studies bounded-diameter classes and periodic or generalized-shift behavior; see Durand et al.~\cite{durand} and Formenti--Grange~\cite{formentigrange}. No new classification theorem is claimed here.

\begin{proposition}[No computable complete diameter cutoff]\label{smc:sl:prop:cutoff}
Suppose a fixed $F$ and computable finite input encodings $e\mapsto x_e$ reduce an undecidable predicate to eventual occurrence of an effective finite local observation, possibly anchored. There is no total computable $B(e)$ such that, whenever the observation first occurs at $h$, every configuration through time $h$ has diameter at most $B(e)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
If such $B$ existed, simulate until a hit, an escape from the bound, or a normalized repeat. A hit answers yes. Escape before a hit answers no by the assumed completeness of $B$. A normalized repeat permits the exact tail decision in Proposition~\ref{smc:sl:prop:bounded}, including anchored observations. One of these events occurs in finitely many bounded-width shapes, contradicting undecidability.
\end{proof}
This does not forbid sparse witnesses with large gaps, unbounded integer values, or an externally varying horizon. It forbids a computable complete geometric cutoff through successful runs. A computable complete time or witness-length cutoff would similarly turn the relevant finite search into a decision procedure.

\wtag\ In \emph{canonical-diophantine-certificates} the finite-search step of this proof is the standard argument of Proposition \texttt{cdc:bd:prop:nobound} (also used at the end of Part~I's Section~\ref{smc:cg:sec:barrier}); the statement, a cutoff on orbit \emph{diameter}, is source~13's.

\subsection{Finite rings and global halting}
On a specified $n$-cell ring, every mass sector is finite, so orbit exploration decides reachability. Its cardinality is
\[
 [z^M]\left(\sum_{a\in Q}z^{w(a)}\right)^n
 \le \sum_{k\le M}\binom nk(|Q|-1)^k.
\]
For triple-channel cap $K$ it is $[z^M](1+\cdots+z^K)^{3n}$, at most $\binom{3n+M-1}{M}$. Fixed $M$ makes the bound polynomial in the \emph{numerical} ring length, not necessarily in its binary encoding length. A reversible ring orbit is a cycle from its start.

A line computation from support diameter $D_0$ is reproduced through a specified horizon $T$ by a ring whose periodic copies have disjoint radius-$rT$ influence intervals; $n>D_0+2rT$ suffices. The zero padding and ring length depend on $T$. This cannot replace the infinite line for all times at once.

An injective dynamics cannot enter an existing fixed point or finite cycle from outside: the cycle already supplies the unique predecessor of each of its points. A reversible simulator therefore reports a halt by an observation while retaining information elsewhere. Stabilization of a finite window, or a departing pulse, must not be called global CA halting.

\section{Decidability at total mass at most two}\label{smc:sl:sec:two}
\begin{theorem}[Two-unit-mass decision theorem]\label{smc:sl:thm:two}
Under the positive-weight, unique-vacuum hypotheses of Section~\ref{smc:sl:sec:diameter}, exact finite-configuration reachability and finite-word occurrence, anchored or anywhere, are decidable from every finite-support initial configuration of total mass at most two. Reversibility is not required. For a fixed CA $F$, these problems have an algorithm polynomial in the input size when finite configurations are sparse lists of nonzero coordinate/state pairs with binary coordinates, and a pattern is an explicit full finite word, whose length counts toward input size. An anchored word has a binary anchor coordinate.
\end{theorem}

The theorem rules out universality by these observation problems at masses zero, one, and two within this model. It does not show that three suffices, or identify a least universal mass. The proof is given in detail because the absolute-position and long-excursion issues are easy to miss.

\subsection{Single particles are finite-state walkers}
Let $Q_j=\{a\in Q:w(a)=j\}$. From a singleton of type $a\in Q_1$ at position $u$, positivity and conservation force the next state to be a single weight-one site. Locality puts it within $r$ of $u$. Thus computable maps
\[
 \alpha:Q_1\to Q_1,\qquad d:Q_1\to[-r,r]\cap\Z
\]
give its next type $\alpha(a)$ and displacement $d(a)$. A type trajectory has a finite transient and cycle. For two hypothetical independent walkers use the product type map on $Q_1^2$. Its transient $\mu$ and period $p$ satisfy $\mu+p\le |Q_1|^2$. At each cycle phase $0\le s<p$ their positions are
\begin{equation}\label{smc:sl:eq:walkers}
 L(\mu+s+kp)=L_s+k\Delta_L,\qquad
 R(\mu+s+kp)=R_s+k\Delta_R,\quad k\ge0.
\end{equation}
Types are fixed within a phase. The displacements over a full product cycle are constants. Reversibility would make the singleton type map a permutation, but no part of the proof needs that simplification.

\subsection{Exact acceleration to the next encounter}
A mass-two configuration is \emph{near} if its diameter is at most $2r$. Every other such configuration consists of two weight-one sites $L<R$ with gap greater than $2r$. No radius-$r$ neighborhood sees both. Quiescence then implies that the next update is exactly the superposition of the isolated updates, as long as the configuration starts far.

Each step changes the gap by at most $2r$. Starting with gap greater than $2r$, the walkers cannot cross without entering the near region. For each affine phase of~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:walkers}, solve
\begin{equation}\label{smc:sl:eq:encounter}
 R_s-L_s+k(\Delta_R-\Delta_L)\le2r,\qquad k\ge0.
\end{equation}
If the drift is negative, the first admissible $k$ is the appropriate integer ceiling, truncated below by zero. For zero or positive drift, the phase either already satisfies the inequality at $k=0$ or never does. Inspect the finite transient too and choose the least resulting physical time. This gives the exact first encounter or proves no encounter occurs.

At that first near time the independently calculated positions are still correct, because the preceding update began far. Stop independent motion there and use the actual CA rule for subsequent behavior. Hypothetical crossings or negative signed gaps after that first time are never used as real dynamics. This boundary is essential for collision correctness.

\subsection{Only finitely many encounter shapes}
There are exactly
\begin{equation}\label{smc:sl:eq:near}
 N_{\mathrm{near}}=|Q_2|+2r|Q_1|^2
\end{equation}
normalized mass-two near shapes: one weight-two site, or an ordered pair of weight-one types at separation $1,\ldots,2r$. Record each near shape together with its time and absolute offset. From a previously unseen near configuration, perform one actual CA step. If the result is still near, continue directly. If it is far, accelerate to its first subsequent near encounter by~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:encounter}, or conclude that this is a terminal forever-far segment.

A weight-two singleton may split, but its next diameter is at most $2r$ and is included. A near pair may merge into one weight-two site. Simultaneous collision, exchange, crossing, and coincident outputs are all handled by the actual CA update rather than a proposed particle collision law. A step starting near has diameter at most $4r$, so every newly departing far excursion starts with gap at most $4r$.

After at most $N_{\mathrm{near}}+1$ near visits, either a normalized near shape repeats or a terminal forever-far segment has been found. Every finite excursion is computed exactly. A repeated near shape gives the translating periodic tail~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:translate-tail}. This proves termination of the compressed orbit construction. If $r=0$, there are no separated-pair interactions, the near-pair term in~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:near} is zero, and each occupied site evolves locally; the same singleton and phase arithmetic handles the case directly.

Mass zero is the stationary vacuum. Mass one is the finite-state walker already described. These complete the orbit descriptions needed for Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:two}.

\subsection{Testing every intermediate time}
Skipping an excursion without checking its intermediate configurations would not decide reachability. On a phase of a far segment restrict the integer $k$ in~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:walkers} to its exact interval, which may have no upper bound. The two types $a_s,b_s$ are fixed.

For an exact target with two weight-one sites $(u,a)$ and $(v,b)$, $u<v$, require the type matches and solve
\[
 L_s+k\Delta_L=u,\qquad R_s+k\Delta_R=v
\]
for a common admissible integer $k$. Incompatible mass or occupied-site counts reject the phase. A weight-two singleton is checked at actual near states.

For a full word on an anchored interval $I=[A,B]$, there are only four possibilities relevant to a far segment:
\begin{enumerate}
\item More than two nonzero symbols, or a weight-two nonzero symbol, cannot match
\item Two nonzero symbols require the two matching type and position equations
\item One nonzero symbol at $u$ requires a matching walker there and the other walker strictly outside $I$
\item An all-zero word requires both walkers strictly outside $I$
\end{enumerate}
Each case is a finite union of intersections of linear equalities and inequalities in one integer $k$. Solve them by interval intersection, ceiling/floor operations, and divisibility. This also treats the mass-one walker by omitting the second position.

For occurrence at an arbitrary translate, anchor any nonzero word at a matching occupied site. Two nonzero word symbols require their specified relative gap and types. With one nonzero symbol the other particle must lie outside the corresponding translated interval. An all-zero finite word always occurs sufficiently far from finite support. Again the tests reduce to one-variable affine arithmetic. An empty word, if allowed, occurs everywhere by convention.

A later translated copy of a repeated near loop may hit an anchored target that the first loop missed. Therefore do not stop at a failed first copy. Expand the finite loop into its phases and apply~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:translate-tail} to every phase, including all intermediate excursion times; the offset arithmetic then searches every later copy. Unanchored queries need only one copy. This establishes correctness of every stated observation decision.

\subsection{Why the fixed-rule algorithm is polynomial}
Fix $F$. Alphabet size, radius, product-type transients and periods, and the number of near shapes are constants. Only the initial far gap may be arbitrarily large. Formula~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:encounter} computes its first encounter, if any, using binary arithmetic; that time is at most $O(g+1)$ with a rule-dependent constant, but the algorithm does not simulate $g$ steps. The resulting coordinates have input-linear bit length.

Every subsequent returning excursion begins with gap at most $4r$. During the constant-length product-type transient and one cycle, gap changes by a rule-dependent constant. If cycle drift is nonnegative and this inspected prefix has no encounter, none will occur. If drift is negative, it is an integer at most $-1$, so the remaining number of cycles to encounter is also bounded by a rule-dependent constant. Thus every returning post-encounter excursion has constant duration for fixed $F$.

There are constantly many distinct near visits before a repeat or forever-far tail. Accordingly the compressed orbit has constantly many affine-phase pieces, and every repeating near loop has constant length. The target tests use numbers of input-linear bit length and a fixed number of arithmetic variables. Scanning the explicitly supplied word and performing those tests is polynomial time. No optimal exponent or uniform complexity bound in the description size of $F$ is claimed. Binary encoding of a gigantic gap is crucially different from listing every empty site in that gap.

This proof resembles classical one-counter reachability: after quotienting translation, a separated pair has one unbounded gap, finite internal control, and exceptional dynamics only near a fixed threshold. The direct argument additionally retains absolute displacement for anchored observations. Haase et al.~\cite{onecounter} provides related one-counter context; it is not cited as stating this precise CA theorem. A targeted search did not establish priority of the exact statement.

\subsection{What particle bounds from the literature do and do not say}
Alhazov and Imai's 2016 paper on particle complexity~\cite{alhazovimai} is directly relevant. Its verified abstract describes finite number-conserving CA with nonnegative integer states and finite total numerical sum, and announces a universal construction with five particles and a sufficiently large finite radius. It also announces a radius-one construction based on two-register machines. The abstract does not assert reversibility. These are finite-mass infinite-line claims, not finite-ring universality.

Only the abstract-level model and upper-bound statements are relied on here; the full construction and any optimality claim have not been verified for this report. In a numerical-state NCCA with weight equal to the state value, there is only one weight-one state. The broader weighted-alphabet model of Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:two} allows multiple internal types of weight one. A small-mass construction in that broader model cannot automatically be quoted as a numerical-state particle bound. Neither the five-particle literature statement nor the mass-two decision theorem establishes a three-particle reversible universal model or a minimum universal mass.
\section{Horizon-free semilinearity at mass at most two}\label{smc:sl:sec:semilinearity}
The low-mass decision argument supports a stronger Diophantine conclusion. Unlike the generic sparse certificate, the following result has no supplied horizon and has fixed witness arity after the rule and its arithmetic description are fixed. It applies to a decidable subclass, so it creates no fixed-arity statement for arbitrary universal computation.

\begin{theorem}[Effective timed and untimed semilinearity]\label{smc:sl:thm:semilinear}
Fix a CA satisfying the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:two}, and fix the ordered nonzero labels of an initial configuration and a target, each of mass at most two. The numerical-coordinate relation
\[
 \mathcal R_F(\mathbf x,\mathbf y,T)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 T\in\NN\ \hbox{and}\ F^T(x)=y
\]
is effectively Presburger-definable. Its projection $\exists T\,\mathcal R_F$ is also effectively Presburger-definable. Equivalently these are effective semilinear coordinate relations, with signed coordinates interpreted by orthants or the canonical natural encoding below. Taking a finite union allows the labels to vary over their finite allowed cases.
\end{theorem}
Here Presburger arithmetic has addition, order, integer constants, and quantification, but no multiplication of two unbounded variables. Semilinearity concerns the numerical coordinates, not a language of their binary digit strings. The proof must show why the initial unbounded gap never becomes a variable period or a variable coefficient in a later phase.

\subsection{A linear guard for the entire independent prefix}
Fix the initial ordered weight-one types at positions $u<v$, with $g=v-u>H_0=2r$. Let $\lambda_i,\rho_i$ be the cumulative independent displacements at time $i$, starting at zero. For the product-type transient $\mu$ and period $p$, put $i_s=\mu+s$ and write
\begin{align}
 t&=i_s+pk,\nonumber\\
 L(t)&=u+\lambda_{i_s}+D_Lk,\qquad
 R(t)=v+\rho_{i_s}+D_Rk,\qquad k\ge0.\label{smc:sl:eq:freephase}
\end{align}
All displayed coefficients except $u,v,k$ are fixed by the rule and initial types. Set $c_i=\rho_i-\lambda_i$ and $D_*=D_R-D_L$. The gap at phase $s$ is $g+c_{i_s}+D_*k$.

Define $\operatorname{Safe}(g,T)$ to mean that every gap at $0\le h<T$ exceeds $H_0$. This ensures that actual motion agrees with the independent motion at time $T$, including a possible first near time. It is already a Presburger statement by quantifying $h$ and expanding its finite phase alternatives. A quantifier-free finite guard in the chosen phase is also explicit.

If $T=i<\mu$, require $g+c_h\ge H_0+1$ for $0\le h<i$. If $T=\mu+s+pk$, first impose those inequalities for all $h<\mu$. For a cycle phase $j<s$, earlier occurrences have indices $0,\ldots,k$; for $j\ge s$ they have indices $0,\ldots,k-1$, an empty set if $k=0$. Minima of these arithmetic progressions give the remaining guards:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
Drift & $j<s$ & $j\ge s$\\\midrule
$D_*\ge0$ & $g+c_{i_j}\ge H_0+1$ & $k=0$ or $g+c_{i_j}\ge H_0+1$\\
$D_*<0$ & $g+c_{i_j}+D_*k\ge H_0+1$ & $k=0$ or $g+c_{i_j}+D_*(k-1)\ge H_0+1$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
These are Boolean combinations of constant-coefficient affine inequalities. At time zero all earlier-index sets are empty, as required. Conjoin the applicable guard, the time equation, the current type matches, and target-coordinate equations from~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:freephase}. A finite disjunction over phases describes every target time before or at first near entry, as well as all times on a forever-free initial flight.

\subsection{First entry with the absolute position preserved}
For $r\ge1$ the first near entry from an initially far pair has two distinct weight-one sites and gap $e\in\{1,\ldots,H_0\}$. A step from gap at least $2r+1$ leaves gap at least one, so a merger or crossing cannot skip this boundary. There are finitely many entry shapes $E$.

For a transient entry time $\tau=i$, impose its Safe guard, phase labels, and
\[
 g+c_i=e,\qquad \xi=u+\lambda_i,\qquad \tau=i.
\]
For a periodic entry at $\tau=\mu+s+pk_0$, impose its Safe guard, $k_0\ge0$, phase labels, and
\begin{equation}\label{smc:sl:eq:firstentry}
 g+c_{i_s}+D_*k_0=e,\qquad
 \xi=u+\lambda_{i_s}+D_Lk_0,\qquad
 \tau=\mu+s+pk_0.
\end{equation}
Here $\xi$ is the absolute leftmost entry coordinate. The guard enforces \emph{earliest} entry, rather than an arbitrary later meeting of hypothetical walkers. Every actual first entry satisfies one such case; conversely the guard and bounded displacement prove that each satisfying case is the real first entry. Thus the full relation between initial coordinates, entry shape, time, and absolute offset is Presburger.

\subsection{Finite rule-fixed orbit templates after entry}
For each normalized near shape $E$ at offset zero, preprocess its orbit by the near/far algorithm of Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:two}. There are only $N_{\mathrm{near}}$ such shapes, each fixed by the rule. Every returning excursion has rule-dependent constant duration; preprocess until a normalized near repeat or a terminal nonreturning free flight. Expand the finite returning flights and one complete recurrent loop when present.

This gives a finite collection of templates, each with fixed occupied-site count, fixed ordered labels, and integer constants $\theta,\pi,\eta_j,\delta_j$, representing
\begin{equation}\label{smc:sl:eq:templates}
 \hbox{elapsed time}=\theta+\pi n,\qquad
 \hbox{site }j=\eta_j+\delta_j n.
\end{equation}
A listed isolated time fixes $n=0$; an infinite template has $n\ge0$ and $\pi>0$. A translating loop has the same displacement for every particle in a phase. A terminal free flight has the finite-state phase templates already used above, possibly with different left and right drifts. The occupied-site order holds on every represented time, by translation for a loop and by nonreturn for a free tail.

Starting $E$ at offset $\xi$ adds $\xi$ to all positions. Compose~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:templates} with~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:firstentry}:
\[
 T=\tau+\theta+\pi n,\qquad
 y_j=\xi+\eta_j+\delta_j n.
\]
Substitution gives affine expressions in the unbounded variables $k_0,n$. There is no product $k_0n$: the entry shape has been chosen from a \emph{finite} set, so every subsequent period and drift is a rule-fixed constant, independent of the initial gap. A finite union over entry cases and templates therefore remains Presburger. Targets before or at entry were already covered; these compositions cover every later target. Overlap of descriptions is harmless for definability.

An initially near input selects one of the same finite templates directly, with its initial offset and $\tau=0$. A mass-two singleton can split and a pair can merge, so one discards mismatched \emph{template} site counts rather than assuming the occupied-site count is conserved. A total-mass mismatch is impossible. Vacuum and mass-one cases have stationary or finite-state affine descriptions. At radius zero, each occupied coordinate is fixed and its label follows a finite map, giving a finite transient and periodic label condition. These arguments prove the timed part of Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:semilinear}; existentially quantifying $T$ proves the untimed part.

\subsection{Effective elimination and canonical signed input}
Standard effective Presburger quantifier elimination converts the constructed formula into a quantifier-free Boolean combination of affine inequalities and fixed-modulus congruences; see Cooper~\cite{cooper}. The effective equivalence with semilinear sets is classical, as in Ginsburg--Spanier~\cite{ginsburgspanier}. These standard results are used for the final arithmetic normal form, rather than assumed as a CA-specific reachability theorem.

Encode each signed coordinate $a$ by natural inputs $(a^+,a^-)$ with
\[
 a=a^+-a^-,\qquad a^+=0\ \hbox{or}\ a^-=0.
\]
This gives exactly one representation of each integer. Include this domain condition and strict ordering of occupied coordinates in the predicate before elimination. With at most two initial and two target sites, there are at most eight natural coordinate inputs, and at most nine external natural inputs when $T$ is retained. For untimed reachability, eliminate $T$ itself before the next compiler. Leaving it as a witness would generally destroy uniqueness when a target is visited at multiple times.

\section{Canonical fixed-arity quartics for the low-mass relation}\label{smc:sl:sec:presburger-quartic}
Here is a complete deterministic compiler from a fixed quantifier-free Presburger predicate $\Phi(\mathbf z)$ on natural inputs. Normalize its atoms to $L(\mathbf z)\ge0$ or $L(\mathbf z)\equiv0\pmod d$, where $L$ is affine with integer coefficients and $d\ge1$ is fixed. Equality becomes two inequalities and strict inequality a shifted weak inequality. Express the Boolean structure by AND, OR, and NOT gates. Let $I,C,G$ be the actual numbers of inequality atoms, congruence atoms, and Boolean gates in the chosen circuit, respectively. Identical nodes may be shared or repeated; the counts refer to the circuit actually used.

\subsection{Inequalities and congruences have unique arithmetic witnesses}
For $L\ge0$ introduce natural $b,s$ with residuals
\[
 b(b-1),\qquad L-(2b-1)s-b+1.
\]
They force $(b,s)=(1,L)$ when $L\ge0$, and $(b,s)=(0,-L-1)$ otherwise. Thus both witnesses exist uniquely for every input, and $b$ is the truth bit even when the atom is false.

For $L\equiv0\pmod d$ introduce six natural witnesses $q^+,q^-,a,h,b,s$ and five residuals
\begin{align}
 q^+q^-,&\qquad L-d(q^+-q^-)-a,\nonumber\\
 a+h-(d-1),&\qquad b(b-1),\qquad a-(2b-1)s-b.\label{smc:sl:eq:congruence}
\end{align}
The first three impose the unique signed Euclidean quotient $q=q^+-q^-$ and remainder $0\le a<d$. The product zero condition gives the unique sign-separated quotient representation; $h=d-1-a$. The last two force $b=s=0$ if $a=0$, and $b=1$, $s=a-1$ if $a>0$. Therefore the congruence truth bit is the affine expression $1-b$. All witnesses are uniquely fixed, including negative quotients and modulus one.

\subsection{Boolean gates and the exact ledger}
For each Boolean gate introduce one natural output $z_g$ and its corresponding residual
\[
 \begin{array}{ll}
 \mathrm{AND}:&z_g-uv,\\
 \mathrm{OR}:&z_g-u-v+uv,\\
 \mathrm{NOT}:&z_g-1+u.
 \end{array}
\]
Inputs are preceding outputs, atom truth bits, or constants zero and one. They are already Boolean, so each gate output is uniquely its correct Boolean value. Add one residual $o-1$ for the final output $o$. Summing the squares of all residuals gives a polynomial of degree at most four with exactly
\begin{equation}\label{smc:sl:eq:presburger-counts}
 K_{\Phi}=2I+6C+G\quad\hbox{natural witnesses},\qquad
 E_{\Phi}=2I+5C+G+1\quad\hbox{residuals}.
\end{equation}
Every input has exactly one complete assignment to the atom and gate witnesses. The final residual accepts it exactly when $\Phi$ is true. Thus the natural fiber is a singleton for true inputs and empty for false inputs. This does not rely on a product of branch equations with unconstrained inactive witnesses.

\begin{corollary}[Horizon-free canonical low-mass certificates]\label{smc:sl:cor:fixedarity}
For each fixed CA and ordered label case in Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:semilinear}, timed and untimed exact reachability admit effectively constructible integer polynomials of degree at most four with zero or one natural auxiliary witness tuple. Their witness arity is the rule- and compiler-dependent constant $K_\Phi$ in~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:presburger-counts}, after effective quantifier elimination and a chosen Boolean circuit. No supplied time bound is required.
\end{corollary}
These constants may be extremely large. The report neither instantiates a quantified-formula eliminator for every possible rule nor claims a small universal numerical bound, efficient uniform preprocessing, or an optimal arity. Total arity is the number of external natural inputs plus $K_\Phi$, at most $9+K_\Phi$ timed or $8+K_\Phi$ untimed for fixed labels. Mass two does not mean two witnesses. Coefficients and their bits, formula length, and Boolean-circuit size remain paid parts of a concrete instantiation.

Uniqueness here comes from the \emph{post-elimination arithmetic compiler}, not from CA determinism: an untimed target may be reached at many or infinitely many times. No corresponding real or rational witness characterization is claimed. No analogous semilinearity or finite-fold statement for arbitrary recursively enumerable sets follows from this decidable low-mass result.

\wtag\ \textbf{A second route, at a higher degree.} Corollary~\ref{smc:sl:cor:fixedarity} rests on the general fact that a semilinear set has a single-fold Diophantine representation, and for that fact the collection already has a sharper statement: Theorem \texttt{cdc:of:thm:singlefoldsl} of Part~XI of \emph{canonical-diophantine-certificates} gives, for every semilinear set, an integer polynomial of degree at most \emph{two}, nonnegative on the real nonnegative orthant, with exactly one natural witness for each member and none otherwise, built in the same way (Presburger quantifier elimination, then deterministic evaluation of the order and congruence atoms, with the comparison of \texttt{cdc:of:lem:unboundedcompare} and a Euclidean congruence gadget, and Boolean gates). The compiler of this section is a second route to that fact, of degree four (squared residuals) rather than an orthant-nonnegative quadratic, which carries the exact ledger~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:presburger-counts}. What is source~13's here is the semilinearity of the low-mass relation (Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:semilinear}), to which either compiler applies. Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:semilinear} is related only loosely to Part~I's rigidity theorem (Theorem~\ref{smc:cg:thm:rigidity}): there a fixed globally nonnegative quadratic forces a semilinear zero set, here low-mass dynamics forces a semilinear reachability relation.
\section{Reproduction and evidence}\label{smc:sl:sec:reproduction}
The accompanying ZIP contains editable LaTeX, the rendered PDF, standard-library Python implementations, coefficient-explicit fixtures, completed finite local tables, structured test receipts, and a SHA-256 manifest. It contains no copies of third-party papers. Source links and exact attribution limits are given in the bibliography and provenance document.

\subsection{What each layer of verification establishes}
The producer compiler constructs each sparse polynomial as a canonical list of integer-coefficient monomials. An export includes the public input/table descriptor, variable and residual ordering, canonical witness values, row aliases, degree and scalar ledgers, and every residual coefficient. Its bound verifier regenerates the declared circuit and compares it before evaluating the witness. This catches deletion of equations, changed input binding, changed row aliases, altered accounting, and malformed scalar types. Merely checking that a supplied witness satisfies some unbound list of equations would be weaker. A caller seeking one particular input must still compare the public descriptor with that intended input; no file can infer an unstated intention.

An independent implementation separately constructs the preferred row-selector polynomials. Its cross-check compares residual coefficient multisets under an explicit wire-name correspondence, not only decoded trajectories or final zero scores. Separate exact dynamics tests compare sparse and dense physical evolution. A third set of source checks verifies the corrected rule schemes, legal source macrosteps, mass-fiber completion, and the false-observer example. Finally, the low-mass audits test encounter acceleration and query arithmetic; they are not exhaustive tests of all CA rules.

Finite tests support the implementations and expose edge cases; the general correctness results rest on the proofs in this report. None of the code constitutes a proof-assistant formalization, an exhaustive literature search, or a test of every natural witness.

\subsection{Selected literal fixtures}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrrr@{}}
\toprule
Fixture & $V$ & $R$ & Multiplications & Additions\\\midrule
Binary three-way collision & 288 & 243 & 1,614 & 1,424\\
Same, orthant-exact & 288 & 261 & 1,830 & 1,559\\
Gap $10^{100}$ & 108 & 88 & 540 & 495\\
Mixed ternary mass & 610 & 370 & 3,694 & 3,287\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
All counts use the literal convention~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:ops}. The huge-gap example has maximum witness bit length 334 and residual-coefficient bit length 333, illustrating that sparse dimension can remain small while geometric values and coefficients grow. The mixed-mass fixture has largest witness bit length four. The orthant collision fixture adds eighteen residuals, exactly $2MT$.

The restricted-row doubling fixture is bound to the full completed local table but uses the declared 97-row simulation subset. It verifies an actual first pulse, rather than merely simulating a source trajectory outside the polynomial. The larger independently replayed $n=2$, $T=41$ case has 257,872 witnesses, 157,683 residuals, 1,263,585 nonzero residual terms, 1,694,649 literal multiplications, and 1,421,268 additions. Its largest witness is 45. Both coefficient-explicit instances are shipped. The smaller $n=0$, $T=7$ instance has 45,712 witnesses, 28,585 residuals, 222,845 residual terms, 299,323 multiplications, and 251,430 additions. Its largest witness is 25. The counts for each are generated and checked by the included script.

\subsection{Audit coverage}
The independent dense/sparse dynamics tests cover 13,200 randomized steps and 73,728 steps across all 36 binary conservative local permutations, for 86,928 comparisons. The independent preferred literal compiler checks 36 instances containing a total of 17,058 witnesses and 9,663 core residuals, and verifies the $2MT$ orthant increment. The earlier factorized independent compiler checks are retained separately with their own ledgers; those results are not relabeled as preferred-backend tests.

The preferred producer/independent coefficient cross-check matches 54 compilers, 21,096 witnesses under explicit renaming, and 11,844 residuals coefficientwise. It additionally checks 20 uniform-loader instances, 24 paid loader-plus-observer instances (12 valid first pulses and 12 correctly rejected earlier pulses), six restricted-row coefficient comparisons, and ten accounting or declaration mutations. Restricted-domain exits and a restricted table omitting the vacuum are rejected. These tests use the final preferred backend, not the older factorized compiler.

The source audit exhaustively enumerates 373 valid three-state source machines under its stated syntax and endpoint conventions, checking 8,672 legal macrosteps with both counters between zero and three. It also checks 3,000 seeded random source machines with 2--12 states for local properties, and 22,004 legal macrosteps on the first 300. Every rule family is exercised. There are 672 startup-signal predecessor tests. The doubling source is replayed for $n=0,\ldots,10$, including exact microtimes, final counters, and persistent result centers. The nonuniversal doubling and false-signal completions contain 17,576 and 9,261 rows respectively.

The independent mass-two arithmetic audit checks 93,663 cases: 13,656 exhaustive and 20,000 random encounter instances; 20,000 anchored-word, 20,000 unanchored-word, and 20,000 exact-configuration queries; and seven large-integer or unbounded cases. A separate encounter regression checks 6,750 far segments and a 100-digit encounter. These are tests of the affine arithmetic used in the proof, not claims about that many distinct CA rules.

The semilinearity component regression checks 1,697,593 cases: 484,788 exhaustive and 1,210,000 random free-prefix guards, four hundred-digit time cases, 81 inequality and 1,452 signed congruence bounded-fiber cases, twelve Boolean truth-table cases, 256 composed-polynomial inputs, and 1,000 single-variable mutations. An independent audit checks another 837,150 arithmetic and actual-CA cases, including 394,740 timed configurations with splitting, merging, recurrent near templates, and terminal free flights. Both audits include exact affine and negative-quotient edge cases. Neither implements arbitrary-rule quantifier elimination or constitutes formal verification.

The release runner regenerates outputs in a separate working directory and compares stable results with the shipped receipts. Its README lists commands, dependencies, and the optional larger reproduction. The manifest detects accidental modification; it is an integrity record, not a replacement for the mathematical or semantic checks.

\wtag\ \textbf{The release in this report.} The programs are shipped as \path{code/13-sparse-lattice-*}, the receipts, generic fixtures and the two completed tables as \path{data/13-sparse-lattice-*}, and the provenance record as \path{13-sparse-lattice-SOURCE-PROVENANCE.md}; the delivered sub-directories \path{replay/}, \path{receipts/} and \path{tables/} are flattened, and the three programs named \path{audit.py} keep their directory in their shipped names (README of this report). The two completed tables (\path{data/13-sparse-lattice-doubling-complete.csv} and \path{data/13-sparse-lattice-false-signal-complete.csv}) are kept byte for byte with their delivered CRLF line ends. The two coefficient-explicit Morita fixtures named above (the $n=0$, $T=7$ and $n=2$, $T=41$ instances, 0.84 and 4.37~MB compressed) are \emph{not} shipped: the shipped generator rebuilds them in about 14~s and 94~s with identical decompressed payloads, and the README gives the commands; their receipts are shipped. The delivered manuscript (six \LaTeX\ files), PDF, README, checksum ledger, packager and one byte-identical copy of the Morita audit are not shipped. The programs need the delivered layout and POSIX (LF) newlines, and must be run on a copy (README).

\wtag\ \textbf{The repository's review.} The programme reviewed this archive in full~\cite{reviewsl}: it found the generic history theorem, the loader and observer, the corrected source interface, the diameter barrier, the mass-two decision proof and the semilinearity argument sound within their stated domains, regenerated both large Morita fixtures, and ran both complete eighteen-stage replays. Its one finding is a low-level exactness defect: the public \texttt{Poly} constructor of \path{sparse_mass.py} accepts floating coefficients and mutable terms, so a hand-built floating residual can round $-1$ to $0$. The high-level compiler and the JSON decoder already require exact coefficients, so the finding refutes no theorem and no exported certificate. The review's repair is the patch \path{sparse_mass_exact_polynomials.patch} in the programme's directory~\cite{reviewsl}; the shipped \path{code/13-sparse-lattice-sparse_mass.py} is the delivered, unpatched file. Two later reductions of the programme bear on this Part's ledgers without changing its theorems: a wire projection of the preferred compiler with a fixed initial configuration~\cite{reviewslproj} removes $T[7M+M(M-1)]$ natural variables and as many rows, leaving $V=T[M(S+7)+4M(M-1)]$ and $R=T[10M+4M(M-1)]$ with quadratic residuals and a bijection of natural zero sets; and a five-witness congruence atom~\cite{reviewcong} lowers the witness count~\eqref{smc:sl:eq:presburger-counts} of the post-elimination compiler from $2I+6C+G$ to $2I+5C+G$ at the same residual count. Both are the programme's, are not re-proved here, and are not gate counts.

\section{Further questions and boundaries}\label{smc:sl:sec:questions}
The strongest next steps are narrowly defined rather than general claims of universality improvement.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Print one safe universal source.} Choose and export a concrete universal counter simulator, retain the history and prime-encoding invariants, and provide its actual $m$, complete local table, valid input encoding, and proof of reachable legality. This would replace the present existence interface with a fully numerical instance
\item \textbf{Compress sparse microtime when structure permits it.} The certificate pays one layer per physical lattice step. Large counter gaps create long round trips. An exact arithmetic macrostep compiler for the source could remove these journeys without changing the finite-mass semantics, but would have to pay for branch tests, timing, and valid intermediate routes
\item \textbf{Separate mass, state, radius, and degree costs.} The row-selector simplification reduces residual count dramatically while retaining $q^3$ witnesses and long sums. Other explicit lookup or collision organizations may trade these resources. None of the displayed counts is a lower bound or minimality record
\item \textbf{Resolve small-particle model comparisons.} Check the full particle-complexity constructions and their precise observation, state-weight, radius, and reversibility conventions before claiming a sharper universal mass threshold. The mass-two theorem is a rigorous exclusion in its stated positive-weight model; it is not a proof of a matching upper bound
\item \textbf{Keep exact targets separate from halt signals.} A reduction to a specified complete target needs its own output-normalization argument. The safe-source pulse interface alone cannot supply it
\end{enumerate}
The construction gives a family of finite-horizon Diophantine certificates with explicit variable growth. Treating $T$ as an additional unknown in a single fixed-arity universal equation, or hiding all horizons behind an unspecified MRDP representation, would be a different result and would not inherit these scalar counts. No such transfer is made for the universal higher-mass sparse family. The horizon-free fixed-arity construction in Sections~\ref{smc:sl:sec:semilinearity}--\ref{smc:sl:sec:presburger-quartic} is instead for the decidable mass-at-most-two subclass and pays its actual post-elimination circuit size.

\wtag\ Two of these questions touch questions elsewhere in the collection. The first is the lattice counterpart of Part~III's literal source: Part~III prints a complete universal conservative signal machine, while source~13 deliberately leaves its safe universal source as an existence construction. The certificate of Theorem~\ref{smc:sl:thm:core} also bears on the question ``Local certificates on adaptive support geometry'' of Part~XVI of \emph{canonical-diophantine-certificates}, which asks for a sparse lattice support checked canonically at a cost proportional to support and boundary, paying for sorting, distinctness and nearest-neighbour tests: source~13 pays exactly those costs (pairwise equality and a bubble-sorting network) for one-dimensional conservative partitioned automata, at size $O(T(M^2+Mq^3))$, which is not proportional to support plus boundary, and not for sandpiles. That question stays open.

\appendix
\section{Part~I: a minimal worked use of the compiler}
\label{smc:cg:app:example}

The following code can be run from the archive root. (\wtag\ ``The archive root'' is the delivered layout, with \path{code/signal_certificates.py}; in this report the module is shipped as \path{code/07-collision-geometry-signal_certificates.py}, so the snippet runs in a copy that restores the delivered name (README of this report).) It builds the
first-collision tournament and constructs a complete certificate without
using an external algebra system.
\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, "code")
from signal_certificates import Machine, compile_skeleton

machine = Machine(
    {"A": 2, "B": 0, "C": -1, "H": 0, "R": 0},
    {
        frozenset(("A", "B")): ("H",),
        frozenset(("B", "C")): ("R",),
        frozenset(("A", "B", "C")): ("R",),
    },
)
skeleton = (((0, 1),),)
cert = compile_skeleton(machine, ("A", "B", "C"), skeleton)
gaps = (1, 1)
slacks = cert.witnesses(gaps)
assert cert.evaluate(gaps, slacks) == 0
assert not cert.accepts((3, 1))  # An earlier BC collision.
assert not cert.accepts((2, 1))  # A simultaneous ABC collision.
print(cert.to_dict())
\end{lstlisting}
The order of rows in the exported matrices is lexicographic and need not
match the order used in the displayed mathematical formula. Row order
changes only the order of slack coordinates, not the represented relation
or the uniqueness statement.

\section{Part~II: replay and build guide}\label{smc:sd:app:replay}
The accompanying archive contains this article in editable LaTeX, the compiled PDF, exact Python scripts, expected receipts, example coefficient dictionaries, and a manifest with file hashes. It contains no third-party source PDFs. The scripts require Python 3.10 or later and only the standard library. Assertions must remain enabled; do not run Python with \texttt{-O}.

From the extracted package root run:
\begin{verbatim}
python3 scripts/run_replay.py
\end{verbatim}
The runner copies the executable scripts into a temporary directory, executes the suites there, compares fresh JSON receipts to the stored expectations, and writes a summary under \texttt{replay-output}. The stored expected receipts and packaged examples are not overwritten. Use \texttt{--output DIR} to select a different result directory. The worked-example receipt also contains its concrete witness and explicit rejection reason for the false final-cut schema.


\wtag\ These are the delivered names. In this report the five scripts are shipped as \path{code/11-signal-certificates-*.py}, \path{build.sh} as \path{code/11-signal-certificates-build.sh}, the two example files and the manifest as \path{data/11-signal-certificates-*.json}, and the four expected receipts as \path{data/11-signal-certificates-expected-*_receipt.json}. The runner looks for \path{scripts/}, \path{examples/} and \path{expected_receipts/} in the parent of its own directory and runs the scripts by their delivered names, so it runs only in a copy that restores the delivered layout (see the README); it writes nothing into the shipped files. Of the archive contents listed above, the editable source is printed as this Part rather than shipped separately, and the compiled PDF and the checksum file \path{CHECKSUMS.sha256} are not shipped (the checksum file was verified, 17 of 17 files, at placement); the manifest is shipped, and its payload list still names the delivered paths and the delivered PDF and article source.

The main executable interfaces are:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{signal\_geometry.py}: exact simulator, full-layer compiler, polynomial evaluation and rational Gaussian rank calculation;
\item \texttt{signal\_sparse.py}: event-coordinate and adjacency-lifetime compiler;
\item \texttt{verify\_additional.py}: separately seeded edge cases and sharp height checks;
\item \texttt{verify\_examples.py}: the article's explicit examples and convention checks.
\end{itemize}
The low-level compilers use assertions to reject malformed schema partitions or structurally impossible collision directions. They are reference mathematical implementations, not hardened parsers for untrusted inputs. JSON residual dictionaries use \texttt{@} for the constant term, \texttt{x0}, \texttt{x1}, and so on for input positions, and the listed variable names for natural witnesses. A certificate evaluates to the sum of the squares of those rows. No expanded polynomial is required to replay it.

To rebuild the article run:
\begin{verbatim}
sh build.sh
\end{verbatim}
A working TeX Live installation with \texttt{pdflatex}, Latin Modern fonts, AMS packages, \texttt{geometry}, \texttt{microtype}, \texttt{booktabs}, \texttt{enumitem} and \texttt{hyperref} suffices. The build script uses fixed document metadata and a fixed source-date epoch; exact PDF byte equality across different TeX distributions is not promised. The mathematical replay receipts are exact and version-independent within supported Python semantics.

The source-supported context is limited to the following claims. All certificate theorems and proofs in this article are supplied here rather than attributed to the literature.
\begin{itemize}
\item Durand-Lose 2009: finite configurations in Definition~1; integer inputs in Section~2; speeds in Figure~4; cyclic-tag simulation and halting in Section~5 and Theorem~5. Common-denominator rescaling is our inference, proved in \eqref{smc:sd:eq:normalize}.
\item Durand-Lose 2007: linear-register event simulation in Section~4 of the author version, especially its ninth PDF page; rational models in Section~5. No Diophantine theorem is attributed to it.
\item Durand-Lose 2006: energy and live-population bounds in Definition~3 and the following paragraph; accumulation qualifications in Sections~2.2 and~6.
\item Ginsburg and Spanier 1966: equivalence of Presburger-definable and semilinear subsets of $\NN^n$, Theorem~1.3, pp.~288--289.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Where the merge had to choose.}
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Base and order.} Source~07 is the base (Part~I): it allows signed rational speeds without a normalization interface, proves more about the chamber, and is the longer manuscript. \item \emph{The common theorem.} Its two statements have different inputs and witnesses, so both are printed, each with its proof. The lemmas of Part~II that coincide with lemmas of Part~I (Lemmas~\ref{smc:sd:lem:denom}, \ref{smc:sd:lem:lifetimes} and~\ref{smc:sd:lem:interior}, Proposition~\ref{smc:sd:prop:semilinear}) open with a \wtag\ note naming their Part~I counterpart, and source~11's proofs are kept as second routes.
